OpenCode 2.0OpenCode 2.0
10/14 20:48 · ▲ 848 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
OpenCode 2.0
OpenCode 2.0 is a major overhaul. It draws on millions of daily users' experience to deliver a system that can flex to everything an agent needs to be. There are quite a lot of new features, performance improvements, and bug fixes but here are some of the more impactful user-facing changes.
The Service
From the beginning OpenCode adopted a client/server model. However, this was not something it took advantage of by default and was mostly used by power users.
In 2.0 this server now automatically runs as a single background process on your machine. When you open up a TUI, the web interface or the desktop app they're all talking to the same central process.
If you're using the programmatic client @opencode/client to make your own scripts, they will autodiscover the local service and just work. This is particularly useful for scheduled tasks. You can also call it via CLI by using opencode api commands.
You can expose it to other hosts and run /pair or opencode pair to connect it to other applications. We have native apps in the works that take advantage of this flow.
The API
The old API grew haphazardly as we and the world collectively learned what agents are and should be. It was bad.
We started from scratch and examined all the weird use cases across the millions of users who use OpenCode every day. It now supports nearly anything you need to be able to do.
OpenCode is also aware of its own API and will call it to do useful things like moving the current session into a git worktree when starting new work.
We've also shipped a new client package @opencode/client that contains clients for vanilla JavaScript as well as Effect.
Code Mode
The models love to write code and OpenCode 2.0 takes advantage of that more than any other coding agent. We've built a minimal JavaScript interpreter in the @opencode/codemode package.
This package ingests MCP servers or OpenAPI endpoints, converting them into functions that the agent can call in a script. It can run these scripts natively without the complexity of Node.js VMs or embedding complicated solutions like QuickJS.
This lets your agent write scripts that can do things like call a tool, map over the results and call another tool. Single round trip instead of calling them one at a time.
All MCP servers in 2.0 are opted into codemode by default. This means you can add a thousand of them with minimal context bloat and your agent will discover and compose them to get things done.
Background Tasks
The latest batch of models are incredibly good at orchestration and it is important for harnesses to let them express the full extent of that capability.
First, we've added background subagents. This allows the primary agent to kick off some work to another one while it continues.
This happens automatically but you can also trigger it manually - even with a different model. It lets you do things like “spawn a background fable subagent to review this code” while you keep prompting.
Second, we've added background shell commands. The shell tool can be invoked asynchronously so long-running work does not have to block the primary agent. It'll be notified when things are complete.
If any foreground shell command or subagent takes longer than you expect, you can hit a hotkey to move them to the background so you can keep prompting.
Plugins
Although OpenCode 1.0 had a plugin system, it never got the attention it needed. To correct that, we forced ourselves to implement nearly every OpenCode 2.0 feature as an internal plugin and there are now 80+ of them. This guarantees that the Plugin API stays well maintained.
We've also promoted TUI plugins from experimental to stable. Since it is all powered by OpenTUI, you can build nearly anything. Here is an example of something useless.
We also spent quite a lot of time ensuring everything in OpenCode 2.0 can be hot-reloaded. So it can write a plugin for itself mid-session and use it right away - we covered the design of this in a previous blogpost.
You can ask OpenCode to build whatever plugin you want and it knows how to. But if you do want to read the docs, they are available here.
The SDK
OpenCode can also now be used as a library with the @opencode/sdk package. This is the exact same code that powers our CLI.
This means you can strip it down and make your own flavor of OpenCode that runs on a server or in specialized environments like Durable Objects. And then you can connect to it from any OpenCode client since it speaks the standard OpenCode server protocol.
It also has an optional Effect-based API.
This one is still in development as things are shifting but you can already use it.
Upgrading
To upgrade the CLI, make sure you're on the latest 1.x release, then run opencode upgrade --major.
To upgrade your desktop app, download the latest here.
There are some breaking changes.
1.0 plugins will not work in 2.0
Server API is completely different
tui.json config is moved to json
There are a lot of other changes but they are backwards

アメリカ人の社会的活動は過去30年で大幅に減少したAmericans' social activity has steeply declined in the past 30 years
10/14 16:09 · ▲ 560 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Americans’ Social Activity Has Steeply Declined in the Past 30 Years
In recent years, there has been extensive discussion of a social decline in the United States, including a U.S. surgeon general advisory in 2023 that declared American loneliness to be an “epidemic.”
Various surveys have tracked America’s social decline over the past few decades. Tracking data from the American Time Use Survey shows that Americans are spending more time alone and less time with friends than they were two decades ago. Perhaps most notably, the American Perspectives Survey has found that from 1990 to 2021, the percentage of Americans with five or more close friends dropped by 25 points, from 63% to 38%.
But while extensive public tracking data show general declines in Americans’ socialization, little, if any, public data show what this looks like for the average American — how the day-to-day activities Americans participate in have changed over the past few decades.
In a new survey, Data for Progress surveyed 1,098 U.S. adults and asked them how many times they engaged in various specific social activities in the past 12 months. These questions directly replicate the DDB Needham Life Style surveys, which tracked Americans’ participation in various activities from 1975-1999 and were referenced in Robert Putnam’s 2000 book “Bowling Alone.” In this analysis, Data for Progress’ 2026 data is compared with the DDB Needham Life Style survey from 1995, which is the earliest year in which full data for all comparison questions was available.
This survey finds significant, double-digit declines in Americans’ participation in key social activities from 1995 to 2026.
The chart below compares the percentage of Americans who report participating in each tested activity at least one time in the past 12 months, between the 1995 and 2026 surveys. We find that Americans’ participation in various social activities has declined by double-digit margins, including attending a class or seminar (-22), entertaining people in your home (-21), attending church or a place of worship (-20), going on a picnic (-19), going to a club meeting (-19), playing cards (-19), going out to breakfast at a restaurant (-13), and doing volunteer work (-12).
A few activities have seen increased engagement: using the internet (+90), playing a home video game (+22), attending an exercise class (+14), going to a pop or rock concert (+13), and taking an airline trip for personal reasons (+5).
Methodology note: This analysis compares two surveys conducted using different methodologies, including differences in constructing sampling frames, modes, and weighting approaches. The findings below should be read as suggestive of trends, not a precise estimate of change. Please see the “Methodology” section below for more information.
One of the steepest declines in the survey is for “entertaining people in my home.” While in 1995, 88% of Americans reported entertaining others at home at least once in the past year, this number has declined to 67% in 2026.
Church attendance has dropped from 74% in 1995 to 54% in 2026.
The percentage of Americans who report having gone on a picnic in the past year has declined from 58% in 1995 to 39% in 2026.
There has also been a 19-point decline in attendance at club meetings, from 45% in 1995 to 26% in 2026.
This analysis adds to existing public data on America’s social decline. Previous reports have found that Americans are spending less time with others and reporting fewer close friends. This new analysis demonstrates that Americans are indeed spending less time in specific social settings, such as restaurants, churches, picnics, parties, bars, and social clubs.
Methodology
The DDB Needham survey was conducted by Market Facts using a stratified quota sampling procedure that begins with a sampling frame acquired from commercial list brokers. The sample oversamples by demographics such as race, gender, and marital status to oversample lower propensity respondents. Finally, the survey contacted individuals via mail.
In comparison, the Data for Progress adult sample was conducted through web panel suppliers and ensured national representativeness via quota fielding and post-stratification weighting.
There is plenty of research on the compositional differences that exist across survey collection modes, and this is likely to affect these results as questions about religiosity and loneliness tend to demonstrate modal differences and social desirability biases. We understand that these factors are at play in our comparison, but find that the magnitude and consistency in trends across measures is a substantial signal about trends in behaviors nonetheless.

中年男性の自殺率が過去最高にSuicide rates highest ever among middle-aged men
10/14 19:03 · ▲ 498 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Suicide rates among middle-aged men are higher than ever, new figures have revealed.
Men in their early 50s were more likely to die by suicide than anyone else last year, according to data from the Office for National Statistics.
There were also increases in suicides among pensioners, with the biggest rise among those aged 75 and older, while the rates among men under 45 fell slightly.
Mark Winstanley, the chief executive of Rethink Mental Illness, said the rate of deaths by suicide remained “stubbornly high”.
He said: “Every suicide is a devastating tragedy, and these figures are a stark reminder that many people are reaching crisis point without getting the help they need.”
The reasons behind suicide were “complex”, he said, but added that many people were “facing significant pressures in their lives, including financial issues, job insecurity, rising housing costs, loneliness and poor mental health”.
Suicide rate highest on record
Mr Winstanley added: “While these factors affect everyone differently, they can contribute to distress and overwhelm, and leave people at risk of suicide.”
The data, which cover England and Wales, showed that 6,162 suicide deaths had been registered in 2025. This was down from 6,190 last year, but the overall rate remained broadly the same at 11.3 deaths per 100,000 people.
The stats watchdog uses deaths per 100,000 people to calculate changing rates while accounting for changes in the size of the population.
Almost a third of the deaths were among men aged 45 to 64, which was up by 49 compared with 2024. The rate increased to 23.8 per 100,000, up from 23.2 the year before and the highest on record since data collection began in 1981.
For men aged 50 to 54 specifically, the rate increased to 28, suggesting this group was at the highest risk.

Muse Spark OpenMuse Spark Open
10/15 17:47 · ▲ 492 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Muse Spark Open is available today and brings leading agentic capabilities to your desktop for the first time on Apple M5 Ultra Mac Studios.
Today, we’re releasing the weights for Muse Spark Open, the most capable US open-weight model to date. Muse Spark Open is a natively multimodal model with 816B total parameters (42B active) and a 1M-token context window. Built for coding and agentic work, it excels at long-horizon tasks, complex software engineering, and multi-step reasoning.
We believe the future is for everyone, and that includes the freedom to run advanced models wherever, including at home. Muse Spark Open is the first model in its class to run on your desktop using two Apple M5 Ultra Mac Studios with the latest MLX framework.
Muse Spark Open is available today through API platforms at Baseten, Fireworks AI, Groq, Modal, Nebius, OpenRouter and Together AI. Model weights are available for download on Amazon SageMaker AI, Google Cloud Model Garden, Hugging Face, and Microsoft Foundry Models.
It runs on leading inference engines like SGLang, vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, Hugging Face Transformers, Apple MLX, llama.cpp, and Unsloth with optimizations for AMD, Apple, Intel, and NVIDIA silicon. Developers can easily further customize the model using the PyTorch framework.
Key Benchmarks
For more details about our evaluations, see the evaluation methodology report.
Optimized for Apple Silicon
Leveraging the latest version of MLX, Muse Spark Open was optimized to run on Mac Studio with M5 Ultra, and together they’re the best way to run a powerful model on your own desktop.
Apple’s unified memory architecture lets the M5 Ultra's integrated GPU use up to 512 GB per Mac Studio, so two machines can hold an entire model. And the chip’s 1.2 TB/s of memory bandwidth is fast enough to read the small slice each token needs. The result is two Mac Studios behave like one supercomputer with a terabyte of unified memory.
We tested a range of agentic workflows running entirely on-device, using Muse Code powered by Muse Spark Open, including shipping real iOS changes to Ice Cubes (an open-source Mastodon client) — bug fixes, new features, full build — and driving DaVinci Resolve end to end, with the model reviewing footage frame by frame to find the strongest moments and crop them vertically.
Available Now
We’re sharing this work because we want everyone to have access to the best models. The open-weight version of Muse Spark ships with custom, auto-tunable Helion GPU kernels in SGLang, delivering 1.03-3.95× kernel-level speedups on NVIDIA and AMD GPUs, and a 3% end-to-end speedup on AMD MI350.
Safety and Preparedness
Releasing open-weight models is part of how we bring the benefits of personal superintelligence to everyone, and part of how safety work advances across the field.
Open weights also carry their own considerations, and Muse Spark Open has additional safety post-training for that reason. The results demonstrate strong safeguards across risk categories measured.
We evaluated the model in line with our Meta Superintelligence Scaling Framework, covering the most severe risks, and it met the thresholds our framework requires for release. Detailed results are in the Muse Spark Open model card, with a full evaluation report available soon.
License
We’re releasing the weights under the Muse Model License with limited commercial terms, which allow many developers to use the model for free.
Get Started
You can customize Muse Spark Open for your use case by leveraging PyTorch’s TorchTitan to further tune the model. To run it on Apple silicon, use MLX, which pulls the weights directly from Hugging Face.
Muse Spark Open is available now through Hugging Face: https://huggingface.co/collections/meta-models/muse-spark-open
MLX Adapter for running Muse Spark Open on Mac Studio.
Hosted Inference Partners: Together AI, Fireworks, Baseten, Modal, Groq, Nebius, and OpenRouter.
Built or published something with Muse Spark Open? Share with us on the Hugging Face Forums — we’d love to see what you create.

オンライン広告が「ゼロクリック」スパイウェア配布に悪用されていると研究者が指摘Online ads weaponised to deliver ‘zero-click’ spyware, researchers say
10/15 12:58 · ▲ 491 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
An Israeli advertising agency has been serving millions of online adverts that allow “zero-click” spyware to be used against targets, researchers at a Canadian university have said.
Citizen Lab, a digital investigations research unit at the University of Toronto, said it believed dozens of devices at the Qatari broadcaster Al Jazeera had been compromised by spyware delivered through ads run by Discover-Tech, which describes itself as a specialised digital advertising agency.
According to Citizen Lab, Discover-Tech operates a software platform called Controlium that allows its clients to “mass-profile and deanonymise” internet users then covertly hack targets with third-party spyware. Unlike infections that rely on people interacting with links or files, the spyware requires no interaction from the victim to be installed, making it a new delivery technique, Citizen Lab says.
The infections happen without a user’s knowledge, and in a matter of seconds, according to Citizen Lab.
“[The online advertising] ecosystem is being weaponised at a massive scale to profile millions of users, then silently infect targets with zero-click mercenary spyware,” the report says.
According to the report, Controlium is able to harvest a user’s identity and IP address when the user is shown a seemingly benign custom-created ad – for example, for a digital game or a telecoms service. If the user has been selected as a target for hacking, a mercenary spyware firm can then send a malicious code containing spyware to their device, the researchers say.
They say that millions of users worldwide have been served ads by Discover-Tech since 2024, including in Yemen, Brazil, Mexico and Czechia.
Related work by the research group Confiant also suggests that Discover-Tech’s ads have been shown to users of the networks of government entities in Europe and the US, as well as the UN, Nato, the international criminal court and the International Atomic Energy Agency.
“It’s disastrous that major ad vendors and organisations apparently were neither able to detect nor prevent the company’s malicious advertising operations for years,” said Wolfie Christl, a researcher at Citizen Lab. “The way the online advertising ecosystem works today is a security threat for states, the private sector, civil society and everyone else alike.”
According to Citizen Lab, Controlium acts as a “facilitator” for third-party spyware firms that have targeted “government officials, journalists, businesspeople, activists, academics and embassy workers”. Citizen Lab claims it can deliver spyware that can access everything on the device of a target, extracting messages, photos and emails, recording calls and secretly activating microphones.
The full extent of the targeting allegedly enabled by Discover-Tech is unknown. Citizen Lab’s findings join mounting evidence that the opaque arrangements underpinning the world of online advertising can be leveraged for surveillance.
“There has been a lot of buzz that online ads can be used to identify and automatically hack targets with pinpoint precision, but now for the first time, we have concrete technical evidence showing this process in action,” said Bill Marczak, a researcher at Citizen Lab.
Discover-Tech has been approached for comment.
When a user navigates to a website that shows advertisements, their details become part of a split-second programmatic auction involving dozens or hundreds of companies. These companies bid automatically to place an ad in front of that user.
These bids are based on the user’s profile, which can include information about their device and data compiled on them by tracking their activity across the web. For example, companies given the profile of a 35-year-old interested in sports betting and crypto might bid to show that individual an advertisement for an energy drink.
There is little regulation on this automated online market, and there is growing evidence that it is being exploited by malevolent actors.
Reuters has reported that billions of dollars’ worth of Meta’s ad revenue comes from ads for scams, illegal gambling, pornography and other banned content. In the US, ICE and other government agencies have bought software that uses ad tech data to track people’s movements.
“Cybersecurity agencies, regulators and lawmakers in the US, Europe and other regions must urgently step in. They must investigate the involved companies and fix the systemic risks that enable malicious ad targeting,” said Christl.

スキューモーフィックなデザインのLinux向けWaylandデスクトップWayland desktop for Linux with skeuomorphic design
10/15 13:23 · ▲ 438 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
English | 日本語
v0.1.2 (BETA) -- v0.1, the first public release, with fixes and an installer for Fedora. See RELEASE-NOTES.md.
I've created a Discord server! You can also report bugs and submit ideas for new features here! link : https://discord.gg/xR5f7Xs49
DEsk is Desktop Environment and desk in one word -- a
name that came from the Reddit community. It used to be called DeskWM:
the commands are now desk, desk-session, desk-settings and so on, and
what was set up for DeskWM (its settings, its data, DESKWM_* variables)
carries over by itself.
A Wayland desktop for Linux in realistic skeuomorphism -- the kind iOS 6 had, where everything on the screen is drawn to look like the real thing. Here the desktop is a desk.
Windows are sheets of paper on a walnut desk. The dock is a drawer, your apps are filed in binders on a bookshelf behind the desk, notifications arrive in a post box, and the system controls are a panel built into the back of the desk. The room is ray-traced, and one desk lamp lights all of it.
Status: beta. DEsk is used day to day on its author's machines and has been checked carefully (see docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, section 9), but this is its first release: expect rough edges, and expect things to change. Your settings and data will be carried forward.
Download the latest release from the Releases page and unpack it (or clone this repository). Then, in its folder, run the line for your system:
Debian 13
sudo sh scripts/install_debian.sh
Ubuntu 24.04 / 26.04 (and Linux Mint, Pop!_OS)
sudo sh scripts/install_ubuntu.sh
Arch Linux (and EndeavourOS, Manjaro)
sudo sh scripts/install_arch.sh
Fedora 43 / 44 (and Nobara)
sudo sh scripts/install_fedora.sh
Then log out and choose DEsk on the login screen.
- It shows what it will install and asks first. --yes takes the suggested
answers; --no-apps installs DEsk without the recommended programs.
- The first login draws the room once: a few seconds on a GPU, longer without one. After that it starts at once.
- To update, get the new release and run the same line again. Your settings are kept.
- Something went wrong? See Reporting a problem.
More options, building by hand and running DEsk in a window are under Build and Run.
Nothing here is a symbol for a thing on a desk. As in the realistic skeuomorphism of iOS 6, each is drawn to look like the real thing set down there -- its material, the light on it, its shadow and the wear of use.
- Windows are paper. Each window sits on a cream sheet with real fibre, thickness and a dog-eared corner. Hold the pointer on the corner and it lifts to show what is printed on the back: Fold (minimize: folded like a letter and left on the desk, under the windows as paper put down is), Unfold / Refold (maximize and restore), and Discard (close: crumpled into a ball and tossed). Dragging an edge pulls more paper out from behind the sheet.
- The drawer is the dock. Pull the brass handle (or press Super+D).
It holds the things you put in it: any app, or a folded window. Out of
the box it holds DEsk's standard programs (below) and a screwdriver that
opens Shell Settings.
- The bookshelf holds your apps (Super+A, or the ALL APPS plate).
Each category is a ring binder whose sheets fan from a single ring.
Take a sheet and it is carried down to the desk, where the app's window
opens on it. At the end of the row stands an ALL APPS index book:
every program from A to Z, with thumb tabs and a line at the top of the
page to type into (input methods via IBus work).
- The post box is the notification centre (Super+N). Letters show the
sender and subject, and a letter taken out lies on the desk.
- The Control Panel (Super+C) is a valve-era machine built into the
back of the desk: real meters, a volume knob, network and Bluetooth
levers, a fader for the room's lamp and one for the screen's own
backlight (on a laptop, through logind; the brightness keys work too),
a key to unlock the power buttons, and the system tray as paper slips
under it (more than fit, and the wheel slides the row along).
- A desk calendar, a desk memo pad and a clock stand on the shelf. The calendar has a notebook of plans and to-dos, and notes are peeled off and stuck onto the day. The memo pad is taken down the same way and written on (IBus works there too); its sheets turn over the wire. The clock shows the seconds small beside the minutes, under two lamps that light only while a camera or a microphone is in use.
- The front door is out in the real world. The lock screen's door has the hour's light -- the sun worked out from your time zone's city, the porch lamp coming on at dusk -- and, if you let it ask open-meteo.com for that city's weather (only its latitude and longitude are sent), the weather: rain darkening the brick and streaking past the lamp, snow settling on what faces up, fog, lightning. And on certain days of the year the door and the clock have something more for you -- there is no setting for it; what it is, you will see on the day (give Shell Settings your bir

AnthropicのScionはMononokeに強く影響を受けているAnthropic's Scion is heavily inspired by Mononoke
10/14 20:30 · ▲ 414 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Anthropic’s Scion is heavily inspired by Mononoke
Update 2026-10-15: I wrote a followup to answer questions and concerns.
Discuss on Hacker News or Lobsters.
Here is Anthropic describing (archive link) yesterday how their new version control system, Scion, uses a concept called “derived data” to make operations like blames more efficient:
Here is me, back in 2022, describing in my Oxide candidate materials how the Mononoke source control server I helped build uses a concept called “derived data” to make operations like blames more efficient:
I think the level of imitation goes well beyond having similar insights1. The fact that Sapling was only credited for “hard-won lessons”, and that Mononoke was not mentioned at all, borders on plagiarism. The post comes off as Anthropic claiming that Claude designed all this, while the reality is that our (extraordinary) team at Meta came up with many of the insights that Claude dutifully applied almost a decade later. (We looked at plenty of prior art for Mononoke and Sapling too, from Google’s SrcFS to write-ahead logs in databases.)
I am very glad that the vision our team had back in 2017-18 was so powerful and enduring — when you go against the grain, you’re always a bit unsure if what you’re doing is genius or madness, and I think Mononoke’s design has stood the test of time. And, to be clear, I do not have a problem with Anthropic copying Mononoke’s design. (This is part of why we put out open source — to benefit humanity through the spread of good ideas.)
But as LLMs continue to assist in designing new systems, they are likely to index heavily on existing designs. Mononoke is open source and in every LLM’s training set. If you ask Opus 5.5 or GPT-6 Astra (thanks Matthew Fowle) about prior art, they both immediately point out the similarities to Mononoke.
Giving credit to those that came before us is now easier — and more important — than ever. There is no longer an excuse.
- For an example of a system with similar insights but many key design differences, see Vicent Marti’s blog post for Cursor. ↩︎

なぜ合理主義者のブログ記事はどれも長すぎるのか？Why is every rationalist blog post way too long?
10/14 06:02 · ▲ 411 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Why is every rationalist blog post way too long?
For my piece on Catholic miracle apologetics, I read (correction: skimmed) a 25,000-word post by rationalist blogger Scott Alexander. My piece, which cited (but does not focus on) his, ended up being a cool 2,700 words. I don't think it would benefit from being longer, and it was the result of me cutting around 70% of the text I initially wrote.
Rationalist bloggers do not generally appear to have this impulse. Eliezer Yudkowsky's "sequences" are over one million words long. Nearly every blog post I have read in this ecosystem, even the rare one that contains some good ideas, is painfully long and extremely tedious. These bloggers tend to spend their time on precisely the wrong things, i.e. adding layers of detail and nuance rather than interrogating the one or two assumptions they make at the start or broadening their philosophical background beyond the collection of texts popular within their subculture.
It seems to me that, in this community, verbosity is a substitute for thoroughness: surely, if someone wrote a lot about something, and is able to demonstrate that by producing a large volume of text, they must have something worthwhile to say about it? Consider when blogger Gwern explained that nicotine, actually, is not addictive, and is good for you, as long as you don't smoke it, over the course of 9,000 words, with over 100 references. It turns out, actually, nicotine is addictive, and is bad for you even if you don't smoke it, as you probably already obviously knew.
In any case, what is missing from this endlessly verbose rationalist writing style is wisdom. Wisdom involves discretion: an intuition for what ideas are and are not worth seriously pursuing, and the ability to determine what does and does not need to be said. This community, broadly speaking, does not seem to value wisdom as a trait at all; the idea that something may be true, valuable, or good merely because it is common sense or inherited tradition is anathema to them. Instead, there is a heavy bias towards that which is unconventional and counterculture, with the amount of ink spilled for heterodox ideas taken as evidence in itself that these ideas are worth taking seriously.
HNAI
ニューラルネットワークはクリーンであるNeural networks are clean
10/15 16:04 · ▲ 390 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
The Short Version
I think I have good news about the nature of neural networks.
After many months studying neurons and circuits in image and language models, I’ve come to believe a network’s own mechanisms are its legible building blocks, and that the outlook for mechanistic interpretability may be far better than I’d feared. The canonical evidence of polysemanticity and superposition that convinced much of the field, including me, that networks pack concepts arbitrarily was misread. What looked like arbitrary packing was mostly a motif I’ll call recursive generalization, which is much less concerning. This means we can study networks directly, instead of first untangling them. Neural networks are already clean.
There are at least two ways to approach mechanistic interpretability. The first is rapid normal science on the network itself: look at a network’s neurons and circuits, study how they relate, watch data flow through them, and run experiments at agentic scale to test explanations. The second starts from a list of hypothesized features and tries to extract them all, designing increasingly sophisticated reconstruction objectives and usually ignoring the original.
The field began with the first approach and fell back to the second because neural networks seemed to be tangled knots. We had canonical examples of polysemantic neurons, the broad name for a neuron that appears to have multiple unrelated jobs or that looks impossible to understand. The most famous one, in an image model, responded to both cat faces and the fronts of cars. And the last layer of the early field’s model organism, InceptionV1, seemed so full of arbitrary mixtures it wasn’t clear how to study it: almost none of the neurons were easily interpretable.
The leading explanation for polysemanticity was superposition. This proposed a new learning principle: when a neural network runs out of neurons, it stops grouping related things together and instead begins folding unrelated things together, simply because they don’t interfere. The result is a kind of knot: the network could have folded in many almost equally good ways, so which things a neuron groups together tells you almost nothing. You have to unfold the knot before you can study anything. In practice that means training a second model – a decomposition such as a sparse autoencoder – to approximate the network as it would look unfolded and studying that instead. If the network really is knotted, there may be no alternative.
One main result is that superposition was the wrong explanation for InceptionV1. Architectural problems made later layers overflow. Weak interpretability tools amplified these problems, making the late layers seem incoherent. Since then, architectural changes designed to help deep layers learn better have had the unintended effect of making them more interpretable. When we first studied InceptionV1, we found almost no interpretable neurons in its last layer. But with these ordinary improvements, and no change to model size or training data, 99-100% of neurons in that last layer are easily interpretable and the model is broadly legible the whole way through. It’s the first non-trivial neural network I feel I mostly understand; you can browse it here. I trained versions of it across a 300× range in size, and it’s broadly legible at every scale. With less room, the network doesn’t fold unrelated things together; its neurons just cover coarser-grained concepts, like one car neuron covering many kinds of car. With more room, that neuron differentiates into many specific cars. So the problem wasn’t the presence of a second learning principle, it was just an ordinary learning failure of the first one, and it’s easy to fix.
The canonical vision examples of polysemanticity and superposition were errors too. In 2019, Chris Olah and I found the circuit that was originally introduced as the superposition phenomenon in Zoom InZoom In: An Introduction to Circuits
Olah, C., Cammarata, N., Schubert, L., Goh, G., Petrov, M. and Carter, S., 2020. Distill. References ↓. It showed a car feature appearing to spread into a set of unrelated dog neurons. It was the only one we showed, and the only one I ever saw. We read it as the network placing the car in a high-dimensional hiding place, for downstream neurons to read back out. But nothing reads that combination. It wasn’t hiding anything; it was ordinary generalization and composition. To my knowledge, no one has published a second circuit with the right shape.
Modern language models are also far more interpretable than generally believed. Anthropic’s Towards MonosemanticityTowards Monosemanticity: Decomposing Language Models With Dictionary Learning
Bricken, T., Templeton, A., Batson, J., et al., 2023. Transformer Circuits Thread. References ↓ (2023), studying a small one-layer model, gave the median neuron a 0 on its rubric out of a possible 14, meaning the rater couldn’t form any hypothesis. The automated interpretability project

Fire at Starlink site in Bulgaria after suspected sabotage in Poland
14.10.2026, 20:51 Uhr
Bulgarian Prime Minister Rumen Radev confirmed on Wednesday that there had been a fire at a facility of the Starlink satellite internet service near the capital Sofia last week.
Two young men holding German passports have been taken into custody in connection with the suspected attack, Bulgarian media later reported, citing investigators.
The satellite network is a strategically important part of Ukraine's defence in the war against Russia's invasion.
The fire had previously been made public by the pro-Western opposition alliance Democratic Bulgaria (DB). Radev said he would not provide any further details in light of the ongoing investigations.
According to DB, 11 of 17 antennas at a Starlink facility near Sofia were deliberately set on fire on October 9. The antennas were used in the war in Ukraine, DB said.
Pro-Western opposition parties said they suspected Russia was linked to the attack.
Two German passport holders in custody
Two men aged 20 and 21, both holding German passports, were detained in Bulgaria on Monday in connection with the suspected attack, several Bulgarian media outlets reported on Wednesday evening, citing investigators.
According to the news outlet Faktor.bg, the two suspects have migrant backgrounds and had legally entered Bulgaria from Greece through the Kulata border crossing.
The website reported that investigators were examining whether the suspects belonged to a larger sabotage network potentially linked to two men arrested in Germany earlier this week over a recent attack on a Starlink facility in Poland.
Sabotage suspected
Bulgarian officials stressed that the Starlink facility near Sofia was privately owned.
Bulgarian Interior Minister Ivan Demerdzhiev told parliament that additional security measures had been introduced at similar facilities, even though responsibility for their protection rested with their owners.
Plamen Tonchev, head of Bulgaria's State Agency for National Security (DANS), said he believed the fire at the Starlink facility was an act of sabotage.
"Such acts of sabotage cannot be ruled out in the future either," he warned lawmakers.
A fire also broke out at a Starlink ground terminal south of Warsaw on September 23.
Two suspected saboteurs accused of carrying out that attack in Poland were arrested on Tuesday in Nürtingen, near Stuttgart in south-western Germany.
The government in Warsaw suspects Russian intelligence services were behind the attack.

銀行が引き受けたばかりのAIデータセンター債務を手放そうとしているBanks are now trying to offload the AI data center debt they just underwrote
10/14 18:32 · ▲ 377 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, SMBC and MUFG are quietly shopping pieces of the AI debt pile they helped build, a sign the lenders closest to the money have started hedging their own bet.
Six months ago, banks were racing each other to underwrite data center loans tied to Oracle, Meta and the rest of the AI buildout. Now some of the same banks are trying to get that debt off their books. JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, SMBC and MUFG have been exploring significant risk transfers, according to a report from the Financial Times. These are deals that let a bank keep a loan on paper while shifting its riskiest slice to outside investors. The banks are doing this because they are bumping up against internal limits on how much AI-linked exposure any one lender can hold.
One case shows how stuck this has gotten. A group of banks including JPMorgan and MUFG has spent more than six months trying to distribute $38 billion in construction debt tied to a Texas and Wisconsin data center project leased to Oracle, Bloomberg has reported. Some lenders are now willing to sell pieces of it to non-bank buyers at a discount, just to move it. Six months is a long time to sit on debt nobody underwrote to hold forever.
Matthew Moniot, co-head of credit risk sharing at Man Group, told the Financial Times the deal sizes involved are "out of scale to anything we've thought about, ever." That is not a hedge-fund manager talking down a trade he wants no part of. It is someone whose job is pricing exactly this kind of risk, saying the numbers no longer fit any model he has used before.
Oracle is the clearest tell. Its five-year credit default swaps, the cost of insuring against the company defaulting, climbed to around 151 basis points in early October, levels last seen in 2009, according to market data cited by Bloomberg. S&P had already cut Oracle's credit rating to BBB-, a single notch above junk, back in July. The company is reportedly targeting more than $40 billion in additional debt and equity this fiscal year. That is on top of the $18 billion in bonds it sold in a single day last September, and the money is needed to keep funding its Texas, Wisconsin and New Mexico sites.
Oracle sued Wisconsin's Public Service Commission over a rule that could force it to post more than $7 billion in collateral for its Port Washington data center campus. The fight helped push S&P to cut Oracle's credit rating to BBB-, one notch above junk, as the company's AI spending outpaces its cash flow. - Oracle Wisconsin data center collateral dispute - utilities regulator $7 billion lawsuit
Demand for that paper has thinned out, too. Bonds from Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Oracle now trade at a premium to similarly rated non-AI corporate debt. Order books for their offerings have shrunk from roughly five times the amount on sale back in February to under two times by July, Bloomberg has reported. That is the difference between a bond that sells itself and one a bank has to work to place.
None of this is happening in isolation. The 10-year Treasury yield hit 5.304% this month, its highest since 2002, after a $39 billion auction cleared at 5.30%, the highest auction yield since 2000. The Federal Reserve's own September meeting minutes named AI-related corporate borrowing as a contributor to that move. Capital Economics argues the bigger driver is plain old inflation and government deficits, not tech company bond sales. Whoever is right about the cause, the effect is the same for anyone trying to finance a data center right now: the risk-free rate they are borrowing against just got a lot more expensive.
Frankly, the mechanics here matter more than the headline number. Banks do not sell down debt they are confident in. They sell down debt when they are worried about concentration, which is exactly what "approaching internal limits" means in plain English: too much of this bank's balance sheet is now one bet on one sector holding up. Spreading $120 billion of AI infrastructure spending across special purpose vehicles was supposed to make that risk easier to swallow. That is the structure Oracle, Meta, xAI and CoreWeave have all used to keep debt off their own balance sheets. Instead the risk is turning up at the banks that financed the SPVs in the first place.
The investors who still want in have gotten pickier about where. Capital is rotating toward data centers leased to investment-grade hyperscalers like Microsoft and Google, and away from weaker credits, Bloomberg has reported. That is exactly why CoreWeave-leased paper prices at 7.00% to 9.875%, against the 5.70% to 7.875% that hyperscaler-leased bonds command, per bond market data compiled by CCIR. A smaller, less capitalized AI infrastructure company trying to raise debt next quarter is now competing for the same nervous pool of lenders as Oracle. Except without Oracle's balance sheet or its existing customer base to point to.
That's the part of this story that does not show up in a single day's stock

ISOをダウンロードしたかっただけなのに、カーネルエクスプロイトを書くことになったWanted to download an ISO, ended up writing a kernel exploit
10/13 12:30 · ▲ 331 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Wanted to download an ISO, ended up writing a kernel exploit
Long downloads kept breaking#
In August 2023 I bought a shiny new Wi-Fi 7 access point, a TP-Link EAP770. The connection was a bit janky, a video would occasionally start buffering, but it didn't bother me much.
I mostly worked on a workstation connected over Ethernet.
When I needed to go somewhere, I'd rsync my ~ to the laptop over Wi-Fi and take it with me.
Those transfers started failing on files larger than a few GiB.
SSH was reporting MAC errors, so I spent a while debugging rsync and SSH.
Everything else worked, changing rsync options made it better or worse, Wireshark showed zero bad checksums and zero retransmissions, and a single UDP stream of random bytes came through clean.
I trusted TCP to deliver the right bytes because that's what the fucking thing is for, so I was sure the problem was in rsync or SSH.
I wrote a wrapper around rsync with retries and moved on.
Later I happened to start using Tailscale for traffic inside my home network, and the transfers stopped needing retries. WireGuard drops a packet if its authentication fails, so the TCP connection inside the tunnel sees a lost packet and retransmits it. Since the transfers worked, I stopped digging.
Zero firmware updates#
The device is an EAP770(EU) v1.0 with a Qualcomm IPQ9574 running Linux 5.4.164 and firmware 1.0.0 Build 20230605 Rel. 71641.
That's the firmware it shipped with, and apparently the last time TP-Link remembered this thing even existed :)
I bought this thing in August 2023, and the Wi-Fi 7 standard was only finalized in September 2024. It has no Wi-Fi Alliance certification, and buying it was stupid of me. PLZ DON'T BUY STUFF UNTIL STANDARDS ARE FINALIZED AND DEVICES ARE CERTIFIED.
In 2025 I noticed that everything else was getting updates while this thing was still on its original firmware. I contacted support in June because the website only had firmware for v2. They couldn't find v1 in their database and asked for photos of the device and box before sending me this:
The EAP770 Version 1 is an End-of-Life product. While it may still be available through some retailers, it is no longer supported with firmware updates.
NICE.
Tracking down the corruption#
In 2026 I decided to switch to NixOS and found that a 128 MiB file from the Nix binary cache on Fastly would fail over HTTP/2 but downloaded fine over HTTP/1.1.
I still don't know why HTTP/1.1 helped; my guess is that HTTP/2 was simply faster.
git clones also failed, and HTTPS connections died with bad record mac.
In March, downloading a NixOS ISO with wget died with a TLS error:
2026-03-05 12:37:18 (31.6 MB/s) - Read error at byte 2722477636/3683778560 (error:0A000119:SSL routines::decryption failed or bad record mac; error:0A000139:SSL routines::record layer failure). Retrying.
I forced HTTP/1.1 and left it alone again.
In September I turned the workstation into a build farm and switched to the laptop, which connects over Wi-Fi.
Wi-Fi was also dropping every day around 11:00, right before my daily call.
After it had died right before the call several days in a row, I decided to open this Pandora's box again and sat down with Opus 5.5 to trace what was happening.
Spoiler alert: the drops turned out to be a separate bug.
I covered the laptop's Qualcomm driver in BPF probes and ran apstats on the AP as root.
The bug is in the closed-source firmware on the laptop's Wi-Fi card; see the update at the bottom.
I had tried enabling and disabling Wi-Fi 7, turning off GRO on the client, and replacing the MediaTek adapter in my Framework 16 with a Qualcomm one. The new adapter and disabling GRO seemed to help, but none of this stopped the corruption.
I captured a failing HTTPS download with tcpdump and found a 1440-byte TCP segment with 64 wrong bytes in a row and a valid TCP checksum.
TcpInCsumErrors stayed at zero.
TCP accepted the segment and handed it to TLS, which rejected the record with bad record mac.
The damaged record wouldn't decrypt, so I couldn't inspect the bad bytes against the expected payload. I switched to raw TCP between a wired machine and the Wi-Fi laptop, sending known data that I could compare byte for byte. The corruption only appeared with several TCP streams running at once, in the LAN-to-Wi-Fi direction. The test found three bad 64-byte blocks in 8192 MiB, each starting with zeros.
A valid checksum on a damaged segment meant something had recalculated it after the corruption, so the question became what on the path rewrites TCP headers. The corruption still showed up after swapping the MediaTek card for the Qualcomm one, so it wasn't the laptop. Wired clients were clean and the CCMP MIC on the bad frames was intact, so it wasn't the cable and it wasn't the air. That left the bridge between Ethernet and Wi-Fi inside the AP. 64 bytes is a cache line on the IPQ9574's cores, which points at a cache or DMA bug rather than a flipped bit. The only thing on that path that both accelerates bridged traffic

AdFection: ターゲティング広告がゼロクリック傭兵スパイウェアの経路にAdFection: Targeted ads turned into a pipeline for zero-click mercenary spyware

📄 本文を読む
Exposing AdFection
How Discover-Tech Turned Targeted Ads Into a Pipeline for Zero-Click Mercenary Spyware
This is the first-ever public confirmation of zero-click mercenary spyware delivered via targeted ads. We call the technique AdFection, and show how it was used to infect devices at Al Jazeera with Candiru’s spyware. We also uncover Discover-Tech, and show how the company helps mercenary surveillance vendors track and hack devices around the world.
Key Findings
- Devices at international news network Al Jazeera were infected with mercenary spyware from Candiru (rebranded as “Integrity Labs”) via targeted online advertisements. We provide the first public technical confirmation of a zero-click mercenary spyware attack delivered via targeted advertising, which we call AdFection.
- The malicious advertisements were purchased by Discover-Tech, an Israeli company that presents itself as a legitimate advertising company, but which we found operates as a facilitator for multiple mercenary spyware partners.
- Discover-Tech operates a platform called Controlium that enables its mercenary partners to de-anonymize, fingerprint, and automatically infect devices the moment an advertisement is rendered — a form of zero-click attack.
- Analysis of four billion Discover-Tech bidding records shows that during a four-month period, the company’s ads were shown on almost 200,000 unique websites and Android/iOS apps in dozens of countries.
- Discover-Tech’s ads also covertly directed millions of devices to contact Candiru’s servers, likely for large-scale tracking and reconnaissance. Discover-Tech is linked to mercenary spyware and intelligence threat actors beyond Candiru, including Intelos and Bindecy.
- A separate threat actor we call DARK CARTOGRAPHER is using Discover-Tech’s infrastructure to exploit vulnerabilities across numerous mobile carriers to link device advertising IDs with phone numbers, mass-deanonymizing internet users. We will detail our findings concerning this threat actor in a separate forthcoming report.
- Discover-Tech has appeared on lists of “certified” and “approved” vendors maintained by Google and advertising industry association IAB Europe, and has served ads via major networks such as Google, Yandex, and Beeswax. Discover-Tech has also leveraged the European General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) consent frameworks to target users within the European Union.
- Discover-Tech’s mass abuse, privacy invasion, and facilitation for mercenary spyware attacks illustrates structural flaws with the online advertising system, which failed to detect or prevent the abuse documented in this report.
- We call on regulators to investigate whether Discover-Tech and Candiru/Integrity Labs violated applicable laws, and make recommendations to lawmakers, defenders, and the advertising industry on how to stop AdFection-style attacks and espionage.
- We disclosed our findings to ad vendors and industry organizations. Google, IAB Europe, Beeswax, Vungle (Liftoff), and Eskimi took action to disconnect Discover-Tech’s access to the digital advertising ecosystem. Other ad vendors we found to be involved in Discover-Tech’s operations did not respond to our request for comment within the timeline provided. We are also actively working with vendors to mitigate DARK CARTOGRAPHER’s mass-deanonymization attack.
This report is co-timed with an investigation by Confiant.
1. Introduction
This section provides an overview of the online advertising ecosystem, and summarizes our findings about Discover-Tech.
The Online Advertising Ecosystem is Ripe for Abuse
When a user sees an ad on an app or a website, it is likely the result of a real-time auction, where advertisers bid on the chance to show that specific user that specific ad. So-called Real-Time Bidding (RTB) auctions, which are hosted on ad exchanges operated by companies including Google, take place hundreds of billions of times a day. In less than a second, the user’s device initiates a bid request, advertisers submit their bids, and the ad exchange determines the winner of the auction and pushes the ad to the user. Besides an image or video, an online ad can also contain code that runs within the app or browser that shows the ad.
Both advertisers and publishers of apps and websites use ad vendors that act as their intermediaries in these auctions. Advertisers typically buy ads via a so-called Demand-Side Platform (DSP), and a Supply-Side Platform (SSP) sells ad inventory on websites and apps.
Malicious actors make use of two interrelated facets of the online advertising ecosystem:
The advertising ecosystem collects a lot of data and enables individual-level targeting: When a publisher of an app or website wishes to show ads, they add code from an SSP to their app or site. This code collects and shares granular information about the user and their device with ad exchanges to solicit bids for the ad slots. This information can include device identifiers and characteristics

Jev 1.14.0を公開、夢想家向けの実験的機能も追加We’re releasing Jev 1.14.0 along with experimental features for dreamers
10/14 21:11 · ▲ 327 · comments →

Jev: For the builders AND dreamers
TL;DR: We're releasing an improved Jev 1.14.0 along with experimental features for our dreamers: images, a new type, and longer context. Enter the danger zone at your own risk. 🔥
Our philosophy
A huge debate we’ve been having is on the balance between reliability and creativity.
To really care about the prod builders, we should be shipping models that meet our quality bar for intelligence and have been battle-tested to the limits of what we can do internally.
On the other hand, there’s no way that our team has a full understanding of what is needed to spark delight in the world and build sick sci-fi. We don’t want to impose our values of extreme reliability on those dreamers.
Our solution: share those dreamer-ready features under “danger zone” flags in our API headers. These are features that will be here to stay for future models, are (as far as we can tell) better than what exists out there, and will continue to get better by leaps and bounds. But as a warning, we think there is still way too much jaggedness for us to vouch for these for production use cases!
See these new capabilities as a continuation of what we’ve built over 2 years of stealth. They’re less ready for automation, but very fun to play with. They’re also an early glimpse into what we’re focused on making reliable enough to meet TypeSafe standards.
Composable Images
Not only are we enabling multimodality for future Jev models, we’re enabling it for the current Jev that you all know and love. Surprise: the Jev you all know and love (jev-1.13.0) was multimodal all along!
Image support is fully composable via a find-and-replace in assets. You can put them anywhere that our JSON interface supports: in the state, instructions, or even criteria! Play around with it, push the boundaries of technology, and let us know where Jev can improve.
As a warning, all models (Jev included) perform worse on multimodality over pure text. Breaking down questions into the simplest possible decompositions helps a lot.
TypeSafe-Danger-Zone-Images: true
Check out docs: docs.typesafe.ai/go/images
Types Beyond Decisions
We’re introducing another new type as well: box. It returns a single bounding box - no type errors allowed. It’s our first type that’s not purely meant for smart-if statements, and is a preview for our explorations beyond simple decisions into what else System One Models can support.
We made huge leaps and bounds on this one recently, so we’re enabling this only for jev-1.14.0 (spoilers).
Check out docs: docs.typesafe.ai/go/box
Size does matter, apparently 💅
We’ve heard you loud and clear on context lengths. Jev actually supports a much longer context length than we’ve enabled in production. We chose to limit it intentionally because Jev, like all models, is less reliable on longer contexts, and this constraint encourages more effective usage patterns in prod.
We aren’t your parents though, so while we strongly recommend limiting context length, you no longer have to:
TypeSafe-Danger-Zone-Long-Context: true
Check out docs: docs.typesafe.ai/models#context-length
Opt-in to help us
We love the community’s desire to help make models better! We’ve added an opt-in to allow you to help find model jaggedness with your queries. We will still not train on your queries, but this does make it easier for us to find issues and help debug your use cases.
jev-1.14.0
We’re also releasing a new version of Jev under jev-preview! Not meant to be flashy but instead be small, subtle improvements to everything. Most things should be a tiny bit better, but we expect the largest gain in multi-lingual support.
Model releases should generally also be strict improvements, but we’re well aware of how much developers hate updating their dependencies. jev-latest will be updated to jev-1.14.0 in a week. jev-1.13.0 will be available for a while. 
We intend to give 60 days warning before jev-1.13.0 is formally deprecated, and 30 days for jev-1.14.0. We’ll release a deprecation policy prior to this.
Measuring Intelligence
As you all know, we dislike public benchmarks. However, we have our own internal best guesses on how to measure the form factor of intelligence that matters most for developers.
We’re trying something new out here: we’re going to treat you guys like adults and share this with you all.
Some nuance
- This measurement is our best guess and will continue to evolve. If ever we show you our internal numbers again, don’t expect them to be consistent.
- Don’t believe it entirely. Always favor your own experimentation and evals with your use cases.
- We’re including only open weight models because we guard our validation set very closely and are spooked out about the data policies of our model providers.
- This is likely biased towards our models. Feel free to ignore our model, and instead focus on the relative ranking of open source models.
What’s next
Lots of people love to ask what kind of sci-fi we’re releasing next. While we are working

数学者が涙を流す理由を私は知っているI know why the mathematicians weep
10/15 13:44 · ▲ 319 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
In the last few months, OpenAI has stunned the mathematical community with the publication of hundreds of math findings. Many of these proofs, which span number theory, theoretical computer science, and logic, had stumped the world’s best mathematicians for decades, or centuries.
The reaction from the math community has been a mix of indignation, astonishment, and sorrow. “Tuesday night, I cried and it woke up my daughter,” said Hugo Duminil-Copin, the recipient of the 2022 Fields Medal, after OpenAI’s October release. “They have annihilated my field of research.”
“I literally cried when I spoke to Wired a few weeks ago,” said Steven Strogatz, a mathematician and author at Cornell University. “People are using language like ‘they’re strip-mining our subject’ or ‘they’re nuking the landscape.’ The feeling is disorientation. I’m discombobulated some days. I’m exhilarated some days. And there is enormous grief.”
I wanted to talk to one of the mourners to understand their technological grief. I wanted to know if theirs was a familiar case of bruised ego or disappointment—akin to, say, a boy who spends an hour building a tower out of blocks and watches his big brother smash the thing to smithereens—or something stranger and more profound.
To be honest, I also wanted to know what it might feel like for me. Today, the mathematicians are weeping, because the machine smashed their tower first. But tomorrow, it may be the physicists, the economists, the illustrators, or the writers who weep over their fallen blocks. I am not one of those AI people who believes that hundreds of millions of jobs will be wiped clean from existence in the next five years. But I am a realist who recognizes that a rapidly improving technology whose output is intelligence is destined to make violent contact with a modern sensibility that prizes human intellect and often equates it with purpose, dignity, and self-worth.
I wanted to know our future. So I called Steven Strogatz.
Craft + Conversation
To prepare you for where my conversation with Steven went, I want to step back from math to ask a question that feels a bit embarrassing in its bigness: What makes humans special?
Some might say intelligence and sociality. We are the smartest creature and Aristotle’s “social animal.” But we share each quality with other species. Crows solve multistep puzzles. Ants congregate and specialize. Elephants appear to mourn their dead.
What sets humans apart is the way these two capacities multiply each other. Human intelligence is unusually suited to imitation and iteration. We imitate our parents and peers with uncanny fidelity, and then we tinker. We copy, and we create. Our sociality spreads this copying-and-tinkering process across groups and generations, so that one discovery becomes the floor for the next. Chimpanzees in West Africa have cracked nuts with stones in the same way for 4,000 years. In roughly the same period, humans went from Babylonian clay tablets to space rockets to machine intelligence. What makes us special, it would seem, is this capacity to compound. Other animals craft and converse, but only humans blend craft and conversation into a culture that builds on itself.
Science itself is both a craft and a conversation. As a craft, science is a mode of thinking; a special way of paying attention to the world; a lens that regards phenomena as something more than mere appearance. To the scientist’s eye, the universe is akin to a cosmic attic of treasure chests, each filled with hidden gemstones that can be pulled into the light if we ask the right questions, find the right keys, turn the right locks.
But science is not a solitary treasure hunt. It is a social experience, akin to a game. Like many games, it is both collaborative and adversarial. Newton famously compared his achievements to “standing on the shoulders of giants,” and a part of science is extending that human pyramid infinitely upward. Feynman preferred to describe science as “the belief in the ignorance of experts”—which is to say, a game of giant-slaying. In both cases, science is a relationship to other people. Whether we’re asking “why, professor?” or insisting “no, that’s not quite it, Mr. Big Shot,” science is not just an act of discovery but a relation to fellow discoverers. Science is a conversation.
End of Conversation
When I called Steven to ask why he cried, he said he wanted to tell me about Archimedes.
Two thousand years ago, the Greek mathematician Archimedes calculated the area of curved objects and pinned down the value of pi by measuring the areas of dozens of tiny polygons that filled out a circle. Writing to the mathematician Eratosthenes about his work on curved shapes and infinity, he wrote that “there will be no lack of discoverers to add to the number of theorems which have fallen to our share.” To Strogatz, this sentence contains the essence of mathematics. I could hear his voice break even as he told this part of the story. “I feel like Archimedes i

Go 1.27でHTTP/1接続の再利用が改善Better HTTP/1 connection reuse in Go 1.27
10/14 16:29 · ▲ 295 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
The Go Blog
Better HTTP/1 connection reuse
Take a look at the following function:
func getStatus(url string) (int, error) {
resp, err := http.Get(url)
if err != nil {
return 0, err
}
defer resp.Body.Close()
return resp.StatusCode, nil
}
It sends a GET request to url and returns the HTTP status code.
Using HTTP/1 (GODEBUG=http2client=0), calling this function 100 times against
index.golang.org takes ~710 milliseconds on Go 1.26
on my machine. On Go 1.27, the same program takes ~150 milliseconds.
That is almost 5 times faster! How is such a drastic performance difference possible?
The cost of establishing HTTP connections
To understand the difference, consider what happens on the network when we make an HTTP request.
Go’s net/http package supports HTTP/1.0, HTTP/1.1, and
HTTP/2. All three run over TCP, and usually over TLS when talking to HTTPS
endpoints. Making a request over a new connection looks something like this:
- TCP handshake (1 RTT): the client sends a SYN packet, the server
replies with SYN-ACK, and the client replies with ACK. Because the
client can send the first TLS message alongside that final ACK,
establishing the TCP connection takes 1 round-trip time (RTT).
- TLS handshake (2 RTT): next, a TLS handshake exchanges encryption keys and verifies the server’s identity; using TLS 1.2, this takes 2 RTT.
- HTTP request: with the keys exchanged, HTTP requests can finally be sent after a total of 3 RTT.
These round trips (plus some CPU time spent on TLS handshakes) add up quickly. In cross-region communications, one-way latencies often reach or exceed the 50 ms (100 ms RTT) shown in the diagram above. Compared to the 300 ms spent just setting up the connection, the HTTP request itself can be cheap to serve, taking only a little over 1 RTT when bandwidth is plentiful or the HTTP response is small.
Even in more favorable conditions, opening a new connection can still be expensive. Our opening benchmark queries index.golang.org, which is distributed worldwide and supports TLS 1.3. This means that latency is relatively low, and the TLS handshake can actually finish in just 1 RTT. Even so, the performance difference is still very noticeable.
Connection reuse
To avoid paying this setup cost on every request, HTTP clients and servers try to reuse existing connections across multiple requests when possible. The mechanics of this differ based on the HTTP version:
- HTTP/1.0: while not part of the official specification, clients and
servers often opt into connection reuse by sending a Connection: keep-alive header. Reuse continues until either endpoint omits the header
or sends a Connection: close header.
- HTTP/1.1: connections are persistent by default, and can be reused for
subsequent requests and responses unless either side declares otherwise by
sending a Connection: close header.
- HTTP/2: multiple requests and responses can be multiplexed over a single TCP connection.
When connection reuse fails
In HTTP/1 (both HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1), multiple requests and responses on the
same connection are sent sequentially on the wire. If two GET requests are
sent to index.golang.org over the same TCP
connection, the responses arrive as a single stream of bytes:
As shown in the diagram, net/http.Get reads and parses
the status line and headers from the byte stream to populate
net/http.Response, then returns immediately. The
response body remains unread on the connection and is consumed only as the
caller reads from net/http.Response.Body.
Because responses are sent back-to-back on the same stream,
net/http can only find the start of the next response after
the current response body has been read to the very end (as determined by the
Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding header).
Now look back at our example program, where we never read resp.Body:
func getStatus(url string) (int, error) {
resp, err := http.Get(url)
if err != nil {
return 0, err
}
defer resp.Body.Close()
return resp.StatusCode, nil
}
Through Go 1.26, when resp.Body.Close is called, net/http
sees that there is still an unread response body. Realizing that it cannot reuse
the connection, it abandons the existing connection and creates new connections
for its next requests, causing it to repeatedly incur the cost of establishing
new connections.
(HTTP/2 does not have this problem. Because it multiplexes streams of frames over a single connection rather than sending responses strictly back-to-back, an unread response body on one stream does not block other requests from sharing the connection.)
Where failed connection reuse hurts
This subtlety of HTTP/1 connection reuse can cause silent performance degradation in seemingly innocuous code.
As in our opening example, a client might only care about an API response’s
status code. However, the API endpoint it is talking to might send bodies that
provide more detailed but ultimately unused information, such as {"success": true, "message": "update processed"}
HNAI
LFM2.5-24B-A2B: 高速なオンデバイスエージェント向けに構築LFM2.5-24B-A2B: Built for fast on-device agents
10/14 23:59 · ▲ 279 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
LFM2.5-24B-A2B: Built for Fast On-Device Agents
Today, we release LFM2.5-24B-A2B, a 24B-parameter reasoning model with just 2B active parameters, built for fast, private agents on-device. It is the largest and final model in the LFM2.5 family, which now spans two orders of magnitude: from LFM2.5-230M to LFM2.5-24B-A2B.
LFM2.5-24B-A2B is competitive with Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, a model with ~50% more total and active parameters, on five of eight agentic and tool-use benchmarks, scoring 48.09 on LiveClaw versus 38.19 and 27.69 on AutomationBench versus 8.72. Because only 2B parameters are active per token, it runs up to 1.7× faster than comparable MoEs on-device, with 184 tokens/s on an Apple M5 Max and up to 21K output tokens/s on a single NVIDIA B300 GPU.
The base (LFM2.5-24B-A2B-Base) and post-trained (LFM2.5-24B-A2B) models are open-weight and available today on Hugging Face and our Playground. Check out our docs on how to run and fine-tune it locally.
What changed since LFM2-24B-A2B
LFM2.5-24B-A2B builds on our previous LFM2-24B-A2B release, with an expanded vocabulary and context window, scaled-up pretraining, and large-scale reinforcement learning. Unlike its predecessor, LFM2.5-24B-A2B is a reasoning-only model that produces an explicit chain of thought before its final answer. We adopted this strategy to leverage its high decode speed and improve answer quality.
Architecture. It keeps the 40 layers, 64 experts per MoE block with top-4 routing, and the same hybrid of gated short convolutions and GQA. The two architectural changes are the context window extension from 32,768 to 131,072 tokens, and the in-place tokenizer expansion, which doubles the vocabulary from 65K to 128K tokens for better non-Latin tokenization.
Pre-training was scaled up from 17T tokens to 33T tokens.
Mid-training has two phases. The first runs 2T tokens at 32K context on curated, higher-quality data weighted toward reasoning, math, code, instruction following, and multilingual text. The second runs 300B tokens at 131K context, weighted toward long context data. Tool calls and multi-turn trajectories appear throughout both phases to prepare the model for agentic workloads.
During teacher specialization, LFM2.5-24B-A2B exhibited well-known reward-hacking behaviors: searching for the final answer in a repository's git history or on platforms like GitHub, PyPI, and Hugging Face. More surprisingly, it even figured out it was in a sandbox, as shown in the examples below.
This behavior looks emergent but might be explained by training data containing similar patterns. We tightened our sandboxes in response, and all reported results below come from sealed evaluation environments.
Benchmarks
We evaluated LFM2.5-24B-A2B across 16 benchmarks covering tool use and agentic workflows, knowledge, instruction following, math, and coding. Despite being the smallest model in total and active number of parameters, it is competitive with, and often outperforms, larger MoEs with ~50% more total and active parameters, such as Qwen3.6-35B-A3B.
LFM2.5-24B-A2B leads or matches the competition on five of the eight tool-use and agentic benchmarks (AutomationBench, BFCLv4, ToolSandbox, LiveClaw, and τ³-Bench Banking), with its widest margin on AutomationBench, where it scores more than three times the next-best model. It also leads on all three instruction-following benchmarks. Larger models keep an edge on knowledge-heavy benchmarks like GPQA Diamond, HLE, and AA-Omniscience, and on coding benchmarks like SWE-Bench Verified (OpenCode) and SciCode.
The results make LFM2.5-24B-A2B an ideal foundation for agents that need to reason, call tools, and run reliably on your device. For knowledge-heavy workloads, we recommend pairing LFM2.5-24B-A2B with retrieval or web search, which plays to its tool-calling strength.
Fast inference everywhere
LFM2.5-24B-A2B ships with day-one support across the inference ecosystem:
- llama.cpp — GGUF checkpoints for efficient edge inference
- MLX — Optimized inference for Apple Silicon
- vLLM — GPU-accelerated serving for production throughput
- SGLang — High-performance GPU serving framework
- ONNX — Cross-platform inference across diverse accelerators
All runs were collected on Pipette, the same benchmarking infrastructure behind Liquid AI's public device performance data.
Edge inference. Due to the efficient LFM2 architecture, LFM2.5-24B-A2B is the fastest of the compared MoEs at reading in prompts and generating answers. It decodes 184 tokens/s on an Apple M5 Max and 114 tokens/s on an AMD Ryzen AI Max+ 395, up to 1.7x faster than the next-best model, while using the least memory.
GPU inference. To demonstrate the competitiveness of LFM architecture we compare the GPU inference throughput to other similarly sized MoE models. We measure GPU throughput (total output tokens / wall time) on a single NVIDIA B300 GPU using a sustained-load setting: at each concurrency level, we continuously maintain the target number of in-flight request

2026年10月15日
各位
ソースネクスト株式会社
当社サイトへの不正アクセスによるお客様情報流出のお詫びとお知らせ
このたび、当社が管理する会員情報に対し、第三者による不正アクセスが行われ、当社に会員登録いただいているお客様の個人情報が外部に流出したことが判明いたしました。
お客様ならびに関係者の皆様に多大なるご迷惑とご心配をおかけしておりますことを、深くお詫び申し上げます。
現在、外部の専門機関と連携し、原因の究明と影響範囲の調査を進めるとともに、再発防止に向けた対策に取り組んでおります。
現時点で判明している事実および当社の対応状況につきまして、下記のとおりご報告申し上げます。

記
- 経緯
2026年10月13日（火）9時頃、当社の会員情報を管理するシステムに対する不正アクセスがあったことを確認いたしました。直ちに外部からの不正な通信を遮断するなどの緊急措置を講じ、10月13日（火）13時36分までに不正アクセスの封じ込めを完了しております。
その後の調査により、第三者から10月9日（金）10時頃から不正アクセスがあり、会員情報取得APIを不正に利用し、当社にご登録いただいているお客様の情報を取得したことが判明いたしました。
- 流出した可能性のあるお客様情報
- 対象件数：22,519,960件
- 対象となる情報：メールアドレス、パスワード、氏名、氏名カナ、住所、性別、電話番号、FAX番号、生年月日、会社名、役職名、部署名、システム固有の識別情報（加入サービス・登録日・ログイン日等）
※上記は流出した可能性のある情報の全項目であり、すべてのお客様について上記すべての情報が流出したわけではございません。ご登録状況により対象項目は異なります。
なお、クレジットカード情報につきましては本件の対象システムには保存されておらず、流出の対象には含まれておりません。
- お客様へのお願い 今回流出した可能性のある情報が悪用され、当社や関係先などを装った不審なメール（フィッシングメール）、SMS、電話、郵便物などがお客様に届く可能性がございます。二次被害を防ぐため、誠にお手数をおかけいたしますが、以下の点にご協力をお願い申し上げます。
- 当社アカウントのパスワード変更について お客様のアカウントを保護するため、現在ログイン時にパスワードの再設定をお願いする措置を講じております。画面の案内に従って速やかにパスワードの再設定をお願いいたします。
- 他のサービスで同じパスワードをご利用の場合 流出した可能性のあるメールアドレスとパスワードの組み合わせを用いて、第三者が他のサービスへのログインを試みるおそれがございます。当社サービスと同じ組み合わせを他のサービスでもご利用の場合は、不正ログインの被害を防ぐため、該当するサービスのパスワードもご変更いただきますようお願いいたします。
- 不審なメールや連絡へのご注意 当社や関係先を装い、偽のWebサイトに誘導して個人情報などを入力させようとする不審な連絡が届く可能性がございます。身に覚えのないメールやSMSに記載されたURL、添付ファイルなどは開かず、不審なWebサイトに個人情報やパスワードなどを入力されないよう十分にご注意ください。
※対象となるお客様には、ご登録いただいているメールアドレス宛に、お詫びとご案内を順次お送りいたします。
- 当社の対応および今後について
当社では、不正アクセス検知後速やかに緊急措置を講じ、10月13日（火）13時36分までに封じ込め対応を完了いたしました。現在も継続して監視を行っており、対応完了後の不正アクセスは確認されておりません。
また、外部の専門調査機関と連携して詳細な調査を進めるとともに、全システムの緊急点検を実施しております。
今後は、システムのセキュリティ対策、監視体制、および情報管理体制を抜本的に見直すことで、再発防止に全力を尽くしてまいります。
本件につきましては、本日10月15日（木）に個人情報保護委員会への報告を完了しており、明日16日（金）に所轄警察署へ被害申告を行う予定です。今後の関係機関による調査・捜査にも全面的に協力してまいります。
今後、公表すべき新たな事実が判明した場合には、当社Webサイト等を通じて速やかにお知らせいたします。
- 本件に関するお問い合わせ窓口 本件に関するお客様からのご質問やご不安にお応えするため、専用のお問い合わせ窓口を設置いたしました。ご不明な点やご心配な点がございましたら、下記窓口までお問い合わせくださいますようお願い申し上げます。 【ソースネクスト 不正アクセス専用お問い合わせ窓口】
- 電話番号：0120-035-116 （フリーダイヤル）
- 受付時間：10時～18時（土日祝日を含む）
- お問い合わせフォーム：https://support.sourcenext.com/fa/support/web/form167.html
- よくあるご質問：https://www.sourcenext.com/support/i/2026/1015_1/
お客様ならびに関係各位にご迷惑とご心配をおかけしましたことを、改めて深くお詫び申し上げます。本件を厳粛に受け止め、再発防止および信頼回復に全社を挙げて取り組んでまいります。

ソースネクストは、第三者による不正アクセスにより、会員情報が外部に流出したと発表した。流出した可能性がある顧客情報は2,251万9,960件。
対象となる情報は、メールアドレス、パスワード、氏名、氏名カナ、住所、性別、電話番号、FAX番号、生年月日、会社名、役職名、部署名、システム固有の識別情報(加入サービス・登録日・ログイン日等)など。流出した可能性のある情報の全項目のため、全ての利用者の各情報が流出したわけではなく、登録状況により対象項目は異なる。なお、クレジットカード情報は対象システムには保存されていない。

📄 本文を読む
連続不正アクセス事案の攻撃手法に関する調査
JPCERT/CCは、2026年10月8日に、国内の複数の組織で相次いで公表された不正アクセスによる個人情報などの漏えい被害に関する注意喚起を公開しました。 JPCERT/CCでは、複数の組織と連携し、攻撃に使用された可能性のあるインフラの調査を進めています。その過程で、攻撃者がAIを利用して、既知の脆弱性のスキャンやAPI経由での攻撃などを実施していたことを確認しました。 こうした攻撃元サーバーはインターネット上で複数確認されており、日本以外の地域向けのWebサービスに対しても攻撃を行っていました。
本記事では、JPCERT/CCが攻撃元サーバーの調査を通じて確認した、国内外のWebサービスなどに対する攻撃の状況を紹介します。
攻撃元サーバーの調査 その1
今回確認した攻撃の特徴の一つは、Android向けアプリケーションの配布ファイル（APKファイル）を解析し、特定したAPIを悪用する点です[1]。 攻撃元サーバー上では、攻撃者がAPKファイルを解析し、関連するAPIにSSRF（Server-Side Request Forgery）などの脆弱性がないかを調査していた痕跡を確認しています。
図1は、調査対象サーバーで確認したファイル一覧の一部です。このサーバーには、日本以外の地域向けに提供されているアプリケーションが保存されていました。
図2は、SSRFの検証に使用されたと考えられるPythonスクリプトです。
このスクリプトはHTTPSサーバーとして動作し、特定のリクエストに対してHTTPステータスコード302のリダイレクト応答を返す処理を実装しています。
攻撃元サーバーの調査 その2
別の調査対象サーバーでは、複数のAPKファイルとともに、Flutterアプリケーションのリバースエンジニアリングに利用されるBlutterや、Androidアプリケーションの解析に利用されるjadxに関連するファイルを確認しました。Blutterの詳細は、Appendix Aをご覧ください。
図3は、このサーバーで確認したファイル一覧の一部です。
このサーバーには、これらのツールに関連するファイルのほか、apk_extract/など、APKファイルの展開に関連する可能性のあるディレクトリも存在していました。また、strings_libapp.txtなど、ネイティブライブラリから抽出した文字列の保存先を示唆する名称のファイルも確認しました。
攻撃元サーバーの調査 その3
今回の攻撃とは直接関係しない別のサーバーでは、Webアプリケーションへの攻撃に加え、MySQL、Jenkinsサーバー、メールゲートウェイなどを標的とする、さまざまな攻撃手法に関連するファイルを確認しました。
図4は、このサーバーで確認したファイル一覧の一部です。サーバー上では、EDRの回避（EDR Bypass）を試みた痕跡に加え、標的のネットワーク内に設置したと考えられるWebシェルなどを確認しました。これらの痕跡から、攻撃者はネットワーク内部への侵入を試みていたと考えられます。
おわりに
JPCERT/CCは、引き続き関係者と連携し、これらの攻撃への対応を進めていきます。 関連する情報（攻撃元IPアドレス、攻撃手法など）をお持ちの場合は、JPCERT/CCまで情報をご提供いただけますと幸いです。 また、攻撃の標的となっている可能性がある組織を確認した場合、当該組織に連絡しています。 JPCERT/CCから連絡を受けた際は対応をお願いいたします。
攻撃インフラ調査チーム
参考情報
[1] NICT：【注意喚起】相次ぐ情報漏洩、その原因の一つは「アプリ解析からのサーバ攻撃」
https://cynex.nict.go.jp/library/261013-01.html
Appendix A：Flutterアプリケーションのリバースエンジニアリングについて
Flutterは、単一のコードベースから複数のプラットフォーム向けのアプリケーションを開発できるフレームワークです。 攻撃元サーバーで確認したBlutterは、Flutterアプリケーションに含まれるDartのAOT（Ahead-of-Time）コンパイル済みコードを解析するためのオープンソースツールです。 Blutterなどを用いたリバースエンジニアリングによって、アプリケーションの内部構造やAPIとの通信処理などを特定できる可能性があります。
2026年6月に公開されたIQCrafterの技術記事では、実際のFlutterアプリケーションを対象に、Frida、Blutter、LIEF、Capstoneなどを組み合わせて解析する手法が紹介されています。 同記事では、libapp.soの静的・動的解析によって、アプリケーションに埋め込まれたRSA秘密鍵やAPIの認証処理、リクエストの署名生成ロジックを特定した事例が説明されています。 さらに、署名処理を再現し、正規アプリケーションを介さずに、独立したPythonクライアントから本番環境のAPIへアクセスできたと報告されています。
また、NerdzLabが公開した技術記事でも、APKファイルやAOTコンパイル済みのlibapp.soを解析し、APIキーや秘密情報、クラス構造、関数アドレスなどを調査する手法が紹介されています。 これらの事例は、Flutterアプリケーションのコードがコンパイルされていても、内部に埋め込まれた秘密情報やAPIの認証処理を特定できる可能性があることを示しています。
アプリケーション内に共通の秘密鍵やAPI認証に使用する秘密情報が埋め込まれている場合、これらの抽出によって、正規アプリケーションを介さないAPIリクエストの生成につながる恐れがあります。 こうしたリスクを踏まえ、次の対策を実施することを推奨します。
- 秘密情報の保護：APIの認証に使用する秘密鍵や、有効期間の長い認証情報をアプリケーションに埋め込まない。
- サーバー側での認証・認可：アプリケーション内の署名処理や難読化だけに依存せず、サーバー側で利用者を適切に認証し、操作対象や操作内容に応じた認可を実施する。
- APIの不正利用対策：レート制限や異常なリクエストの監視を実施し、正規アプリケーション以外からのアクセスも想定する。
- SSRF対策：外部URLの内容を取得するAPIでは、アクセス先を制限し、リダイレクト後の宛先も検証する。
参考情報：
- Reverse Engineering a Flutter Mobile App: A Complete Security Research Methodology（IQCrafter）
- Reverse Engineering a Flutter App: How Secure Is Your Data, Really?（NerdzLab）
- I Reverse Engineered a Banking App With a Million Downloads（Medium）
- Blutter（GitHub）
